\subsection{LQ Triple Interactions}
The LQ self-interactions involving three LQs are given below. 

\begin{center}
\begin{table}[!ht]
    \centering\begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{4.5cm} m{8.5cm}} 
    
    \multicolumn{2}{c}{Triple Interactions} \\
    \hline
        Diagram  & Feynman Rule \\
        \hline
    
        
        \vspace{5px}
        \includegraphics[height=2.25cm]{5-7-1.png}&
        
        \vspace{5 px}
        $-\ 2i A_{1\tilde{2} \tilde{2}} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_a2}
        \left(\epsilon^{ij3} \ W^{-1/3*}_{n_bi}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_cj} \right)$  \newline
        $+\ \frac{i v}{\sqrt{2}} Y_{123} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_a1} \left( \epsilon^{i2j}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_bi}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_cj} \right)$  \newline
        $+\ \frac{i v}{\sqrt{2}} Y_{1\tilde{2}3} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_a2} \left( \epsilon^{i2j}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_bi}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_cj} \right)$\newline
        $-\ i v Y_{1\tilde{2}3} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_a3} \left( \epsilon^{ij3}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_bi}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_cj} \right)$\newline
        $-\ 2i v Y_{\tilde{2}33} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_a3} \left(\epsilon^{1ij} \ W^{-1/3*}_{n_bi}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_cj} \right)$ \vspace{5px}\\
        
    
        










        

        
        \vspace{5px}
        \includegraphics[height=2.25cm]{5-7-2.png}&
        
        \vspace{10px}
        $-\ i A_{\tilde{1} 2 \tilde{2}}
        \ W^{-4/3*}_{n_a1}
        \left(\epsilon^{ij3}\ W^{+2/3*}_{n_bi}
        \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_cj}\right) $  \newline
        $-\ i v Y_{\tilde{1} 2 3} 
        \ W^{-4/3*}_{n_a1}
        \left(\epsilon^{i2j}\ W^{+2/3*}_{n_bi}
        \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_cj}\right) $
         \newline
        $-\ \sqrt{2}i  vY_{2 3 3}
        \ W^{-4/3*}_{n_a2}
        \left(\epsilon^{i2j}\ W^{+2/3*}_{n_bi}
        \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_cj}\right) $ \newline
        $-\ \sqrt{2} i v Y_{\tilde{2} 3 3} 
        \ W^{-4/3*}_{n_a2}
        \left(\epsilon^{1ij}\ W^{+2/3*}_{n_bi}
        \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_cj}\right) $ \vspace{5px}\\

        






















        
        \vspace{5px}
        \includegraphics[height=2.25cm]{5-7-3.png}&
        
        \vspace{5px}
        $-\ i A_{\tilde{1} 2 \tilde{2}} \ W^{-4/3*}_{n_a1}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_b2}$ \vspace{1px}  \newline
        $-\ \frac{i v}{\sqrt{2}} Y_{1 \tilde{1} 2} \ W^{-4/3*}_{n_a1}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_b1}$ \vspace{1px} \newline
        $+\ i v Y_{1 2 3} \ W^{-4/3*}_{n_a2}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_b1}$
         \vspace{1px}\newline
        $+\ \frac{iv}{\sqrt{2}} Y_{\tilde{1} 2 3} \ W^{-4/3*}_{n_a1}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_b3}$\vspace{1px}  \newline
        $+\ 2i v Y_{2 3 3} \ W^{-4/3*}_{n_a2}\ W^{-1/3*}_{n_b3}$ \newline \\

        










        \hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}
\end{center}

\clearpage
\subsection{LQ Quartic Interactions}
\label{sec:LQQuartic}
In the following, the Feynman rules for the LQ self-interactions involving four LQs are listed. If $n_a$ or $n_b$ are equal to zero, $W^q_{n_a n_b}$ should be set to zero (i.e.~the corresponding term neglected). 

\begin{center}
\begin{table}[!ht]
    \centering\begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{4.5cm} m{8.5cm}} 
    
    \multicolumn{2}{c}{Quartic Interactions} \\
    \hline
        Diagram  & Feynman Rule \\
        \hline
            
        
        \includegraphics[height=3.5cm]{5-8-1.png}&
        
        \vspace{5px}
         $+i \sum_{e \in \{\tilde2, 3\}}\left(Y^{(1)}_{e} \ \delta_{c_1 c_2} \delta_{c_3 c_4} + Y^{(3)}_{e} \ \delta_{c_1 c_4} \delta_{c_2 c_3} \right)  $ \newline
         $\ W^{-1/3}_{n_a n_e}
         \ W^{-1/3*}_{n_bn_e} \ W^{+2/3}_{n_cn_e} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_dn_e}$  \newline
         
         $+i \sum_{(e,f) \in \left\{(1,2), (1,\tilde{2}),(1,3),(2, \tilde2),(2,3),(\tilde{2}, 3) \right\}}\newline \left(Y^{(1)}_{ef} \ \delta_{c_1 c_2} \delta_{c_3 c_4} + Y^{\prime (1)}_{ef} \ \delta_{c_1 c_4} \delta_{c_2 c_3} \right)  $ \newline
         $\Big(W^{-1/3}_{n_an_{e}}
         \ W^{-1/3*}_{n_bn_{e}} \ W^{+2/3}_{n_cn_{f}} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_dn_{f}}$ \newline
         $+W^{-1/3}_{n_an_{f}}
         \ W^{-1/3*}_{n_bn_{f}} \ W^{+2/3}_{n_cn_{e}} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_dn_{e}}\Big)$ \newline 
         
         $+i \left(Y^{(3)}_{2 \tilde{2}} \ \delta_{c_1 c_4} \delta_{c_2c_3} + Y^{\prime(3)}_{2 \tilde{2}} \ \delta_{c_1 c_2} \delta_{c_3 c_4} \right)  $ \newline
         $\ W^{-1/3}_{n_a2}
         \ W^{-1/3*}_{n_b2} \ W^{+2/3}_{n_c1} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_d1}$  \newline
         
          $+\sqrt{2}i \left(Y^{(3)}_{\tilde2 3} \ \delta_{c_1 c_4} \delta_{c_2c_3} + Y^{\prime(3)}_{\tilde2 3} \ \delta_{c_1 c_2} \delta_{c_3 c_4} \right)  $ \newline
         $\Big(W^{-1/3}_{n_a3} W^{-1/3*}_{n_b2} \ W^{+2/3}_{n_c2} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_d3} \newline
         + W^{-1/3}_{n_a2} W^{-1/3*}_{n_b3} \ W^{+2/3}_{n_c3} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_d2} \Big)$  \newline
         
          $+i \left(Y^{(3)}_{\tilde2 3} \ \delta_{c_1 c_2} \delta_{c_3c_4} + Y^{\prime(3)}_{\tilde2 3} \ \delta_{c_1 c_4} \delta_{c_2 c_3} \right)  $ \newline
         $W^{-1/3}_{n_a2} W^{-1/3*}_{n_b2} \ W^{+2/3}_{n_c3} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_d3}$  \newline
         
         $-i \left(Y_{1223} \ \delta_{c_1 c_4} \delta_{c_2 c_3} + Y^\prime_{1223} \ \delta_{c_1 c_2} \delta_{c_3 c_4} \right)  $ \newline
         $\ W^{-1/3}_{n_a1}
         \ W^{-1/3*}_{n_b3} \ W^{+2/3}_{n_c1} \ W^{+2/3*}_{n_d1}$ \vspace{5px}\newline
        
         (more of this diagram on next page) \newline\\

         $W^{-4/3}_{n_a2} W^{-4/3*}_{n_b2}$ \newline 
         \vspace{5px} \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

         $-\sqrt{2}i \left(Y^{(3)}_{\tilde{2}3} \ \delta_{c_1 c_4} \delta_{c_2 c_3} + Y^{\prime(3)}_{\tilde{2}3} \ \delta_{c_1 c_2} \delta_{c_3 c_4} \right)  $ \newline

\clearpage
\section{Conclusions}
\label{sec:Conclusions}
In this article we provided the complete scalar LQ Lagrangian, including all interactions with SM fermions, gauge bosons and the Higgs. After EW symmetry breaking the mass terms of the Lagrangian are diagonalized, leading to mixing among different LQ representations. We present all Feynman rules, including for the first time the LQ-LQ-LQ(-Higgs) and LQ-LQ-LQ-LQ interactions. On the computational side, we provided a FeynRules model file containing the complete scalar LQ Lagrangian, as well as a Mathematica notebook to access it. The export to MadGraph and FeynArts is possible and the corresponding files are provided, rendering this a powerful tool for the automatization of studies of scalar LQ phenomenology.



\appendix






\clearpage
\section{Charge-Conjugate SM Fermions}
\label{sec:appendix}

\subsection{Treatment of Charge-Conjugate SM Fermions}
Our treatment of interactions involving charge-conjugate SM fermions is based on the method of Ref.~\cite{Denner:1992vza}. It uses the properties of the charge-conjugation matrix $C$ (introduced in Eq.~(\ref{eq:Cmatrix}))~\cite{Denner:1992vza, Nieves:2003in}


\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
	C^\dagger &= C^{-1} \,, \\
	C^\intercal &= -C \,, \\
	C \Gamma_i^\intercal C^{-1} &= \eta_i \Gamma_i \equiv \Gamma_i^\prime \,,
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:Cequations}
\end{equation}


with $\Gamma_i = 1, i\gamma^5, \gamma^\mu, \gamma^\mu \gamma^5, \sigma^{\mu \nu}$ and 


\begin{equation}
\eta_i =     \begin{cases}
      +1, & \text{ for } \Gamma_i = 1, i\gamma^5, \gamma^\mu \gamma^5 \\
      -1, & \text{ for } \Gamma_i = \gamma^\mu, \sigma^{\mu\nu}
    \end{cases}
\end{equation}


to derive two equivalent sets of Feynman rules for internal propagators, external lines and vertices involving (anti-)fermions. The first set corresponds to the expressions 
$\bar{\psi}_1 \Gamma_i \psi_2$ that appear in the Lagrangian, the second set to their charge-conjugate analogues $\bar{\psi}_2^c \Gamma^\prime_i \psi_1^c$. The two are equivalent 


\begin{equation}
\bar{\psi}_1 \Gamma_i \psi_2 = \bar{\psi}_2^c \Gamma^\prime_i \psi_1^c \,.
\end{equation}


This double allocation allows for an intuitive treatment of interactions involving charge-conjugate fermion fields. While the underlying principle is the same, our notation differs from Ref.~\cite{Denner:1992vza}. We use two types of arrows on the fermion lines (black and white ones) instead of additional arrows next to them. This avoids potential confusion with momentum arrows as well as having clashing fermion arrows. We proceed by stating the recipe for calculating Feynman amplitudes for processes involving charge-conjugate fermions, which we demonstrate using an example. The Feynman rules for diagrams featuring charge-conjugate SM fermions only are given in \ref{sec:CCFeynman}. 

\clearpage
\subsubsection*{Prescription for the Evaluation of Feynman diagrams} 
\label{sec:CCprescription}
The amplitudes for specific processes are determined according to the following prescription:

\begin{itemize}
\item Draw all Feynman diagrams for a given process. Two fermion lines with clashing arrows (i.e.~one line ending in a fermion and the other in its charge-conjugate) may be connected by inverting the direction and the colors of the arrows for one of the two lines. It does not matter which of the two lines are inverted. 

\item For each internal propagator, external line and vertex write down the appropriate analytic expressions proceeding opposite to the fermion flow. The Feynman rules for normal fermions (black arrows) are given in Sec.~\ref{sec:SMInteractions}, the rules for charge-conjugated fermions (white arrows) in \ref{sec:CCFeynman} and the rules involving both in Sec.~\ref{sec:LQfermions}. 

\item Multiply by a factor $(-1)$ for every closed loop.  

\item Multiply by the permutation parity of the spinors in the obtained analytical expression with respect to some reference order.
\end{itemize}


The evaluation of the bosonic parts of a diagram proceed as usual. 

\subsubsection*{Example} 
\label{sec:CCexample}
In order to exemplify our Feynman rules, we calculate the Feynman amplitude for the process $e\nu_\mu \to \Phi_1^{-1/3} \Phi_2^{-2/3}$ with an up quark $t$-channel contribution. A similar example is given in Sec.~4 of Ref.~\cite{Denner:1992vza}, which allows for a direct comparison of our notation to theirs. For simplicity, we neglect the mixing among the LQs and take the SM fermions to be massless. \\

\begin{figure}[ht!]
     \centering
     \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.3\textwidth}
         \centering
         \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{Example1a.png} \newline
         \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{Example1b.png}
         \caption{}
         \label{fig:Example1a}
     \end{subfigure}
     \hfill
     \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.3\textwidth}
         \centering
         \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{Example2.png}
         \caption{}
         \label{fig:Example1b}
     \end{subfigure}
     \hfill
     \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.3\textwidth}
         \centering
         \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{Example3.png}
         \caption{}
         \label{fig:Example1c}
     \end{subfigure}
        \caption{Feynman diagrams for the process $e\nu_\mu \to \Phi_1^{-1/3} \Phi_2^{-2/3}$ involving a $t$-channel up quark. The up quark fermion lines with clashing arrows shown in the diagram on the left can be combined by inverting the fermion arrows (color and direction) of one of the two subdiagrams. This yields the two equivalent diagrams on the right.  }
        \label{fig:Example1}
\end{figure}

The Feynman diagram is shown in Fig.~\ref{fig:Example1}. Fig.~\ref{fig:Example1a} shows two parts of the diagram with clashing fermion line arrows for the up quark. In order to combine the two parts, the fermion arrows of one of them have to be inverted. In Fig.~\ref{fig:Example1b}, the arrows of the upper part are inverted, in Fig.~\ref{fig:Example1c} the ones of the lower part. In the following, we will derive the analytic expressions for both of them, showing their equivalence. Choosing the reference order $(1,2)$ for the external states, the Feynman amplitude for the diagram in Fig.~\ref{fig:Example1b} reads


\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
i \mathcal{M} &= \bar{v}^{(s_1)}(\vec{p}_1) \left(+i Y_{1, 11}^{LL} \text{P}_\text{L} +i Y_{1, 11}^{RR} \text{P}_\text{R}   \right) \frac{-i\slashed{q}}{q^2 + i\epsilon} \left(-i Y_{2, 12}^{RL} \text{P}_\text{L} \right) u^{(s_2)}(\vec{p}_2) \\
&= -i \frac{Y_{1, 11}^{RR} Y_{2, 12}^{RL}}{q^2 + i\epsilon} \bar{v}^{(s_1)}(\vec{p}_1) \ \slashed{q} \text{P}_\text{L} \  u^{(s_2)}(\vec{p}_2)\,.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}


The amplitude for the diagram in Fig.~\ref{fig:Example1c} is


\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
i \mathcal{M}^\prime &= (-1) \bar{v}^{(s_2)}(\vec{p}_2) \left(-i Y_{2, 12}^{RL} \text{P}_\text{L} \right) \frac{i\slashed{q}}{q^2 + i\epsilon} \left(+i Y_{1, 11}^{LL} \text{P}_\text{L} +i Y_{1, 11}^{RR} \text{P}_\text{R}   \right) u^{(s_1)}(\vec{p}_1) \\
&= -i \frac{Y_{1, 11}^{RR} Y_{2, 12}^{RL} }{q^2 + i\epsilon}  \bar{v}^{(s_2)}(\vec{p}_2) \ \slashed{q} \text{P}_\text{R} \  u^{(s_1)}(\vec{p}_1)\,,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}


where the factor $(-1)$ is added since the order of the external states is now reversed. The two amplitudes are equivalent, since 
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
i\mathcal{M} &\propto \bar{v}^{(s_1)}(\vec{p}_1) \ \slashed{q} \text{P}_\text{L} \  u^{(s_2)}(\vec{p}_2) = q_\mu \bar{v}^{(s_1)}(\vec{p}_1) \ \frac{\gamma^\mu - \gamma^\mu \gamma^5}{2} \  u^{(s_2)}(\vec{p}_2) \\
&= q_\mu  u^{(s_2)}(\vec{p}_2)^\intercal \ \frac{\gamma^{\mu \intercal} - \left(\gamma^{\mu} \gamma^{5} \right)^\intercal }{2} \  \bar{v}^{(s_1)}(\vec{p}_1)^\intercal \\
&=  q_\mu  u^{(s_2)}(\vec{p}_2)^\intercal \ C^{-1} \frac{-\gamma^{\mu} -  \gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{5}  }{2}C \  \bar{v}^{(s_1)}(\vec{p}_1)^\intercal \\
&= q_\mu \bar{v}^{(s_2)}(\vec{p}_2) \  \frac{\gamma^{\mu} +  \gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{5}  }{2} \ u^{(s_1)}(\vec{p}_1) =  \bar{v}^{(s_2)}(\vec{p}_2) \ \slashed{q} \text{P}_\text{R} \ u^{(s_1)}(\vec{p}_1) \propto i\mathcal{M}^\prime\,,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}


where we have used Eq.~(\ref{eq:Cequations}) and


\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u^{(s)}(\vec{p}) &= C \bar{v}^{(s)}(\vec{p})^\intercal\,, \\
v^{(s)}(\vec{p}) &= C \bar{u}^{(s)}(\vec{p})^\intercal
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}


for spinors $u, v$, which can be derived from Eq.~(\ref{eq:Cmatrix}). This equivalence is true for all Feynman diagrams involving charge-conjugate fermions, as argued in Ref.~\cite{Denner:1992vza}.

        $\\

===== END PAPER =====

You are benchmarking automated extraction of a BSM physics model from its paper, to be compared against the physicist's own FeynRules implementation.

MODEL: SLQrules (SimpleExtensions) — FeynRules DB entry based on arXiv:2105.04844.
PAPER: the paper's LaTeX source: equations, fractions and roots are exact, included verbatim above between the PAPER markers; it is authoritative.
FORMAT: write a complete FeynRules .fr add-on model file yourself (M$ModelName, M$Information, M$InteractionOrderHierarchy, IndexRange declarations for new indices, M$Parameters, M$ClassesDescription, the Lagrangian terms and one LTotal). It is loaded on top of SM.fr, included verbatim above between the SM.fr markers.

TASK — extract the COMPLETE new-physics content:
1. Read the paper. Identify EVERY new particle beyond the Standard Model: spin (S/F/V), colour rep (singlet/triplet/sextet/octet), SU(2) rep, electric charge Q and/or hypercharge Y, mass parameter. COMPLETENESS IS SCORED — a model with several new states (multiplets, mass eigenstates, generations) must include all of them as separate ParticleClass entries (or class_members).
2. Identify the new coupling/mass/mixing parameters (External unless clearly derived).
3. Write the main new-physics Lagrangian terms (FeynRules/Mathematica syntax and idioms).

FILE RULES: use FeynRules syntax for every value (rationals -1/3, decimals 1500.); SM add-on => no M$GaugeGroups block; the indices Colour, Gluon, Generation, SU2D and SU2W are declared by SM.fr; any OTHER index (a colour-sextet index, a new-generation index) needs `IndexRange[Index[X]] = NoUnfold[Range[n]];` with the correct size; every class gets a unique label S[n]/F[n]/V[n] with n >= 100; SelfConjugate -> False for complex fields (distinct antiparticle), True for real/Majorana fields.

OUTPUT: your FINAL message must contain exactly one fenced code block tagged mathematica containing ONLY the complete .fr file (no commentary inside the fences). Set M$ModelName = "SLQrules_gen".

NO TOOLS: this run has no file, shell, network or code tools. Everything you need is in
this message: the Standard Model file SM.fr (the file this add-on is loaded on top of:
take field names, index conventions and hypercharges from it) and the paper. Do not ask
for anything; do the extraction and write the model.

## Physics-completeness rules (apply to every model)

1. Free-field terms for every new particle class. Write a named kinetic + mass term for each new field even when the paper shows only L_int. Use the mass symbol declared in the class `Mass -> {M, value}`. Add the term to the total Lagrangian sum.
   - Real vector (gauge singlet): `LXkin := -1/4 FS[X, mu, nu] FS[X, mu, nu] + 1/2 MX^2 X[mu] X[mu];` (the explicit form `-1/4 (del[X[nu], mu] - del[X[mu], nu])^2` is equivalent).
   - Complex vector: `-1/2 FS[Xbar, mu, nu] FS[X, mu, nu] + MX^2 Xbar[mu] X[mu]`.
   - Complex scalar: `DC[phibar, mu] DC[phi, mu] - Mphi^2 phibar phi`. Real scalar: `1/2 del[phi, mu] del[phi, mu] - 1/2 Mphi^2 phi^2`.
   - Dirac fermion: `I psibar.Ga[mu].DC[psi, mu] - Mpsi psibar.psi`. Majorana fermion (`SelfConjugate -> True`): `I/2 psibar.Ga[mu].del[psi, mu] - 1/2 Mpsi psibar.psi`.

2. Explicit EFT cutoffs. When the paper writes an operator with `1/Lambda^n` (or `1/M^n`, `1/f^n`), declare each distinct cutoff as an External parameter in GeV with the paper's benchmark value, e.g. `Lam == { ParameterType -> External, Value -> 1000., BlockName -> CUTOFF, InteractionOrder -> {QED, -1}, Description -> "EFT cutoff [GeV], Eq.(N)" }`. Write the factor `1/Lam^(d-4)` verbatim in the term, with `d` the operator mass dimension you computed in the self-audit. Keep the paper's dimensionless couplings (kappa, lambda, c_i) dimensionless with the paper's benchmark defaults. Do this for the term and for its `HC[...]` partner. Only if the paper's own implementation absorbs `kappa/Lambda^n` into one coefficient may you do the same; then the parameter Description must say "= kappa/Lambda^n, mass dimension -n, units GeV^-n" and the default must equal the paper's numeric `kappa/Lambda^n`. Never leave a dimensionful coefficient with an unexplained O(0.1) default.

3. Charge conjugation. When the paper writes a fermion with a charge-conjugation superscript (`psi^c`, `\bar{psi^c}`, `psi^T C`, or a Majorana mass eigenstate that enters a chiral current with the opposite chirality), write it as `CC[psi]` (and `bar[CC[psi]]` for the conjugated bar). Never write the bare field. If the paper's benchmark sets that term's coefficient to zero, either drop the term or write it with `CC[...]`; never keep the term with the conjugation dropped. Use the class field with its Generation index (for example the SM class `vl[ff]`), not a class member like `ve[nn]`, because FeynRules reads an index on a class member as a spin index.

4. SU(2) doublet contractions. Use the SM.fr conventions the add-on loads on: `Phi` has Y = +1/2, `LL` has Y = -1/2, `QL` has Y = +1/6, `Phi[1] = -I GP`, `Phi[2] = (vev + H + I G0)/Sqrt[2]`. Contract a doublet with an anti-doublet on a shared index: `LLbar[sp, ii, ff] ... Phi[ii]`. Contract two objects of the same conjugation type (`LLbar` with `Phibar`, `QLbar` with `Phibar`, i.e. any term the paper writes with `Htilde = i tau2 H*`) with `Eps[ii, jj]` on two distinct indices: `LLbar[sp, ii, ff] ... Phibar[jj] Eps[ii, jj]`. Never contract `LLbar[ii]` with `Phibar[ii]`. Model the SM Yukawas: `QLbar.dR Phi[ii]`, `LLbar.lR Phi[ii]`, `QLbar.uR Phibar[jj] Eps[ii, jj]`.

5. New U(1) charges. Do not copy a paper's charge table literally when the paper's Yukawa equation and its table use different hypercharge conventions (for example the paper effectively uses Y(H) = -1/2). Re-derive the new-U(1) charge of every SM field, including the Higgs, from the requirement that all SM Yukawas and all new Yukawas are invariant under SM.fr conventions. Report which sign you chose and why. Do not put quantum numbers on a `SelfConjugate -> True` class; put a chiral charge on an Unphysical chiral class instead.

6. Read only the paper. Do not open any cached or reference `.fr` file for the same model. If you see one, say so in the output and do not use it.

7. Fractions and roots in extracted equations. The paper text is machine-extracted. A displayed fraction or radical can arrive flattened: the numerator, the fraction bar and the denominator become separate lines, so `A / Sqrt[B]` and `A Sqrt[B]` look identical. For every coefficient that contains a fraction, a square root or a power:
   - If a LaTeX source file (`*_source.tex`) is available, read the `\frac{...}{...}` and `\sqrt{...}` there and take it literally. The LaTeX is authoritative over the flattened text.
   - Otherwise cross-check the placement against a second equation of the paper that depends on the same coefficient (a partial width, a cross section, a limit, or a small-parameter expansion) and name that equation in the self-audit. A coupling `g/Sqrt[1-x]` gives a width proportional to `1/(1-x)`; a coupling `g Sqrt[1-x]` gives a width proportional to `(1-x)`.
   - If neither resolves it, choose the form that reproduces a numerical value the paper states, and say so.
   Never silently pick one placement.

8. Mass mixing. When the paper diagonalises a mass mixing between a new state and an SM state (Z-Z', h-S, W-W_R, nu-N), write the mass eigenstates the paper uses: declare the mixing angle (External, or Internal with the paper's formula) and express the gauge eigenstates through it in `Definitions` of Unphysical classes. Do not leave the mixing in a comment.

## FeynRules and UFO construct rules (each one broke a compile or a MadGraph import in earlier runs)

9. Non-fundamental colour representations. If a field carries a colour index other than the triplet `Colour` or the adjoint `Gluon` (for example a sextet), declare the index range AND add the representation line FeynRules needs, before the field declarations: `AddGaugeRepresentation[SU3C -> {T6, Sextet}];` for a sextet (schema: put both lines in `raw_preamble`; also keep the `index_decls` entry). Write covariant derivatives on the whole field, `DC[Phi, mu]`, never with an explicit colour index inside `DC[...]`.

10. Names. Class names, parameter names and index names must not be Mathematica built-ins or FeynRules symbols: never `N`, `C`, `D`, `E`, `I`, `K`, `O`, `HC`, `CC`, `FS`, `DC`, `del`, `Eps`, `Ga`, `ProjP`, `ProjM`, and never a name SM.fr already declares (`H`, `Phi`, `Z`, `W`, `A`, `G`, `ee`, `gs`, `gw`, `sw`, `cw`, `vev`, `MZ`, `MW`, ...). No primes or punctuation in `ParticleName`/`AntiParticleName` (`W'` is not a valid UFO name; write `Wp`, `Wpbar`). Use class indices of 100 or more for new classes.

11. Interaction orders. Every new coupling parameter (Yukawa, portal, quartic, new gauge coupling, dimensionless EFT coefficient) carries an `InteractionOrder`: `{NP, 1}` for a new-physics coupling, `{QED, 1}` or `{QCD, 1}` only when it is literally that coupling. Declare `M$InteractionOrderHierarchy` with every order you use (for example `{{QCD, 1}, {QED, 2}, {NP, 1}}`). A coupling with no order makes MadGraph reject the UFO ("Some couplings have '1' order").

12. Tensor parameters. Give every indexed parameter explicit per-component values (`yN[1,1] -> 0.1, yN[1,2] -> 0, ...`; schema: `value_rules`). Never use a pattern rule such as `X[i_?NumericQ, j_?NumericQ] :> ...` in `Definitions`: FeynRules exports the pattern verbatim as a UFO parameter name and MadGraph fails with an unrelated "invalid syntax" error.

13. Flavour structure in matrix form. Write generation-space couplings as matrix products between class fields, `uqbar.VCKMR.Ga[mu].ProjP.dq`, `LLbar.yN.NR`, `ISbar.Ga[mu].ProjM.IS`, not as explicit index sums `VCKMR[ff1, ff2] uqbar[ff1, cc]...dq[ff2, cc]`, and never nest one generation index inside another. Explicit-index forms leak FeynRules internals (`ConvertSpinToString`, `MakeIdenticalFermions`) into the UFO.

14. One declared total. Define exactly one Lagrangian term named `LTotal` that sums every other term you define, and make sure every other term appears in that sum. Do not define two alternative totals. Do not emit a term that contains no field (a pure constant): FeynRules exports it as a vertex with no particles.

15. Declarations. Every field used in a term is declared; every parameter used in a term is declared with a numeric default; every `SelfConjugate -> True` class has no `QuantumNumbers`; a Majorana mass term is `-1/2 M psibar.psi`.

## Mandatory self-audit table (produce it BEFORE the model)

For every Lagrangian term you emit, output one row of this table. Then fix any row that fails before you write the model.

| term name | fields in the monomial | operator mass dimension d | coupling symbol | coupling mass dimension (must be 4 - d) | 1/Lambda power present (must be d - 4, or "n/a" if d <= 4) | Q sum | Y sum | SU(2) contraction (singlet? which Eps or shared index) | SU(3) contraction | sum of each new U(1) charge (one column per U(1), must be 0) | lepton/baryon number sum (if the paper assigns them) | CC[] used where paper writes psi^c (yes/no/n/a) | fraction/root placement checked against (equation, LaTeX line, or "n/a") | Hermitian partner (HC[] or self-conjugate) |

Rules for the table:
- Field mass dimensions: scalar 1, vector 1, fermion 3/2, `FS[...]` 2, `del`/`DC` 1.
- Every Q sum, Y sum, and new-U(1) sum must equal 0. Use the charges you declared in `QuantumNumbers` and the SM.fr charges for SM fields. If a sum is not 0, the term is wrong; fix the contraction or the charge before you continue.
- If d > 4, the row must show `1/Lambda^(d-4)` and an External cutoff parameter, or a Description that states the absorbed dimension and units.
- Add one row per new particle class that confirms its kinetic + mass term exists and is in the total Lagrangian sum.
- Add one row per non-fundamental colour representation that confirms the `AddGaugeRepresentation[...]` line is present.
- Add one line under the table that lists every `SelfConjugate -> True` class and confirms it carries no `QuantumNumbers`.
- Add one line that lists every class, parameter and index name you chose and confirms none is a Mathematica built-in, a FeynRules symbol, or an SM.fr name.
- Add one line that names the single total Lagrangian and lists the terms it sums.
- Add one line that states whether any reference or cached model file was read (must be "none").
